Guidestar-Free Adaptive Optics with Asymmetric Apertures
uses Light aberrations bend unpredictably through an asymmetric aperture, and the resulting patterns encode the distortions; machine learning decodes these patterns to infer what the aberrations are, then optics corrects them.
why it works Asymmetric apertures break the lens symmetry, encoding phase information into measurable intensity patterns. Inverting those patterns through phase retrieval recovers the hidden distortion, making a wavefront sensor unnecessary.
the deeper reason
Asymmetric telescope openings create distorted star images that contain hidden information about what light waves are doing as they travel through the atmosphere. When an opening is shaped unevenly, the blurry image it makes encodes phase information—the wave timing—that a symmetric opening would discard. A machine learning model learns to read these clues from real photographs taken through unknown air distortions, then predicts what corrections a deformable mirror should apply. This transforms photography into wavefront sensing without needing an artificial guide star, letting cameras see through atmospheric blur using only natural scene structure.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Telescopes and microscopes struggle to see clearly through turbulent air or cloudy glass — the atmosphere or optical imperfections scramble incoming light waves. We want to correct this distortion in real-time and in software, recovering sharp images of distant objects without needing artificial reference beacons (guidestars) placed in the sky.

why it's hard

Measuring how much the light has been distorted requires knowing either the original light pattern (which you don't have for natural scenes) or a bright reference star at a known position (which may not exist or be too faint). The trick is getting the wavefront measurement from the blurry image alone, using only the quirky shape of the telescope's opening.

what they do

They use an asymmetric telescope pupil (an oddly-shaped opening instead of a circular one) that encodes wavefront distortions into measurable patterns in the image. Two neural networks then infer the optical distortion from these patterns and predict what the true image looks like. A deformable mirror at the pupil plane applies the computed correction optically, closing the loop in real-time.

why it works

An asymmetric pupil breaks the symmetry of how light spreads in the image, so the blurry pattern itself now contains information about the distortion — like a broken mirror shape tells you how light was bent. Neural networks learn this relationship from training data, so correcting distortions that haven't been seen before still works reasonably well.

the payoff

The system corrects optical aberrations without a guidestar, uses 100× fewer images, and needs 1000× less computation than older phase-retrieval methods. It enables sharp imaging of natural scenes through unknown obstacles (dust, haze, or fog) with only a camera and a controllable mirror.

A Two-Millisecond Passthrough Headset for Perceptual Studies
uses End-to-end latency measures the delay between real head motion and display update; perceptual studies measure how human perception changes as this delay shrinks, revealing threshold sensitivities in real tasks.
why it works Catadioptric optics physically curve light rays to achieve perspective-correct viewing. No computational reprojection is needed—the optics themselves perform the transformation, eliminating the delay.
the deeper reason
Latency in virtual reality breaks the real-time connection between your head movement and what you see; above a certain threshold, your brain detects the delay and the illusion of presence collapses. This passthrough headset uses a camera-based optical design that displays reality with only 2 milliseconds of delay—fast enough that the eye-brain connection feels immediate. By keeping this delay below perception thresholds, the system enables true studies of how small latency changes affect user comfort and task performance, filling a gap where faster devices simply couldn't measure.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

In virtual reality, the delay between when you move your head and when the display updates (latency) makes you feel disconnected and dizzy. Lower latency makes VR feel more real and responsive. Current headsets have 12-60 ms latency; understanding whether even lower latencies matter for user comfort and performance requires a device that can test it.

why it's hard

Modern VR systems use clever tricks (pose prediction, late reprojection) to hide latency, but these tricks only work down to about 12 ms. Testing true latencies below that requires new hardware that doesn't need those tricks — a much harder engineering problem.

what they do

They built a video passthrough headset called Camsicle with two cameras and a special mirror-lens system (catadioptric design) that displays the real world with only 2 milliseconds of end-to-end latency. The cameras capture what you see, the system processes it instantly, and the display shows it with virtually no delay.

why it works

By using cameras and mirrors instead of prediction hacks, there's no accumulated error — the user sees the physical world nearly as if the headset weren't there. This true low-latency passthrough is the key to testing whether sub-12-ms latencies actually matter for user preference, rather than just theory.

the payoff

User studies with 57 participants show that 2 ms and 14.3 ms latencies are preferred over 23 and 29 ms in a ball-catching task. The platform enables perceptual research on latency thresholds that was previously impossible, grounding subjective VR comfort in measurable physical quantities.

Woodstock: Interactive Modeling of Fungal Wood Decay
uses Wood decay spreads via fungal colonization and moisture image-generating; simulate how fungi consume material (changing local stiffness), how water flows anisotropically through grain, and how cracks grow when stress exceeds weakened tissue.
why it works Fungal decay couples biology with physics: fungi spread where moisture guides them, and their feeding weakens structure. Simulating both processes together captures the feedback that makes realistic decay patterns emerge.
the deeper reason
Fungal decay of wood is fundamentally a competition for chemical resources governed by how fungi spread through wood fiber and how moisture enables chemical breakdown and structural fracture. The simulation tracks three coupled processes: how fungal colonies advance through the grain, how chemical defenses deplete as fungi consume them, and how moisture-driven pressure causes specific fracture patterns. By modeling wood as a volumetric material with grain-aligned structure and tracking resource consumption plus water flow together, the system captures why wood hollows in specific patterns and why environmental moisture drastically affects collapse, grounding appearance in physical cause.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When fungi decay wood, they don't just eat away randomly — they break down specific chemicals (lignin, sugars, defenses), and the wood's moisture and grain structure determine how fast and where the decay spreads and causes structural failure. Simulating this process realistically would help visualize how trees rot and inform timber engineering.

why it's hard

Wood decay couples biology (fungal spread, chemical degradation), fluid flow (moisture moving through grain), and mechanics (wood fracturing and hollowing) in complex ways. Each domain has different physics, and they strongly influence each other. No prior method unified them.

what they do

They represent wood as a 3D volumetric model with explicit grain alignment, internal moisture, and tissue health states (intact, colonized, consumed). They simulate three coupled processes: anisotropic spread of fungal colonies (following grain and moisture), consumption of wood resources, and stress-driven fracture patterns (driven by moisture and material loss).

why it works

Tracking moisture, fungal spread, and material failure together lets each influence the others correctly. Fungal spread follows grain (anisotropic), so it doesn't just make a sphere around the infection point. Moisture and chemical consumption weaken wood locally, leading to realistic cuboid fracture patterns (characteristic of rot) rather than smooth erosion.

the payoff

Simulations realistically capture the progression of rot: fungal colonization spread, characteristic cube-shaped fracture patterns, trunk hollowing, and environmental moisture effects on structural stability. The rendering shows volumetric decay as a realistic 3D process, not an abstract animation.

Uncertainty-aware geometry processing on Gaussian Process Implicit Surfaces
uses A probabilistic surface model (Gaussian Process) represents uncertain geometry as a cloud of plausible shapes; compute surface operators (slope, curl, curvature) by averaging over that cloud, weighting by how likely each shape is.
why it works The Kac-Rice formula converts the hard problem of averaging over uncertain surfaces into solvable integrals of smooth functions over a fixed domain. This translates uncertain geometry into computable expected quantities.
the deeper reason
Real shapes captured from noisy sensors do not have one true surface; they have a cloud of plausible surfaces consistent with the measurement noise. This framework extends classical surface mathematics—the rules for computing the downhill direction and smoothness—to work on this entire cloud at once, using the Kac-Rice formula to convert the intricate probability problem into computable integrals over space. By doing geometry processing on probabilistic surfaces instead of pretending one measurement is truth, the system naturally handles measurement ambiguity and produces robust results on noisy data.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When you scan a 3D shape with sensors (cameras, LiDAR) or reconstruct it from partial data, there's always uncertainty — ambiguity about the exact surface. Most geometry processing assumes a single, definite shape, throwing away that uncertainty. The goal is to do useful operations (smoothing, curvature, flow) while acknowledging and using the uncertainty.

why it's hard

Classical geometry processing (computing gradients, Laplacians, running smoothing algorithms) assumes a crisp, deterministic surface. But if the input is uncertain, a single fixed surface can mask important ambiguities. Building math for uncertain surfaces from scratch is non-trivial.

what they do

They use Gaussian Process Implicit Surfaces, a probabilistic representation that stores a distribution over plausible shapes instead of one fixed shape. They define analogs of classical operators (gradient, divergence, Laplacian) that work on this distribution, computing expected values using the Kac-Rice formula (a tool from random surface theory).

why it works

Gaussian processes naturally encode uncertainty, and the Kac-Rice formula lets you compute integrals and derivatives over random surfaces by embedding them into a fixed volumetric domain. This converts an intractable problem (differentiate a probabilistic surface) into an integral over a volume, which is computationally tractable.

the payoff

You can now do geometry processing (like smoothing or curvature estimation) that accounts for input noise and ambiguity rather than pretending the input is perfect. This bridges classical surface equations and modern probabilistic shape representations, enabling principled handling of noisy or incomplete scans.

Spatially Accelerated Winding Numbers for Curved Geometry
uses Winding numbers tell if a point is inside curved geometry by measuring rotation around it; precompute moment summaries in a spatial tree, then use Taylor approximations for distant curves and exact integration for nearby ones.
why it works Precomputed moments in the hierarchy bound far-cluster contributions analytically via Taylor expansion; nearby NURBS use exact evaluation. This split gives fast approximation far away and exact accuracy near boundaries.
the deeper reason
The generalized winding number is a scalar field that determines whether a point lies inside or outside a curved boundary, and computing it usually requires expensive surface integrals. This method reduces the integral analytically to just one ray intersection test and a sum over edge signatures, cutting computation dramatically. By building a hierarchical spatial index that stores precomputed moment data for NURBS surface clusters and adaptively subdividing curves to improve spatial partitions, the system computes the field sub-linearly while preserving sharp features near the actual boundary.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Winding numbers answer a fundamental question: is a point inside or outside a 3D shape? This seems simple for clean, closed surfaces, but real meshes are messy — they have gaps, overlaps, and curved boundaries. A robust inside-outside test is essential for simulation, texturing, and boolean operations.

why it's hard

Direct evaluation of winding numbers on large, complex meshes is slow, so spatial acceleration is needed. Existing fast methods approximate by clustering primitives far from the query point. But they only work on discrete meshes (triangles, points), not smooth curves or patches.

what they do

They extend winding number acceleration to curved geometry (NURBS curves and trimmed patches) using a Bounding Volume Hierarchy that stores precomputed moment data at each node. Queries use fast approximations for far clusters but directly evaluate nearby curved primitives, preserving geometric accuracy.

why it works

Precomputing moments (integrals of basis functions) at hierarchy nodes enables accurate approximations for distant clusters without refitting. Direct evaluation near the query preserves fine details (like thin features) that would be lost with purely approximate methods. The two-tier strategy keeps asymptotic complexity low while maintaining accuracy.

the payoff

Winding number queries on curved geometry (NURBS) achieve sub-linear complexity while preserving geometric features, enabling containment queries on arbitrary curved shapes — not just triangle meshes. This is useful for procedural design and simulation on parametric surfaces.

Fast and Exact Winding Numbers for Triangle Meshes
uses Winding numbers measure net rotation around a point to test if it's inside; reduce the surface integral analytically to a single ray-3D model test plus a sum over boundary edges, orders of magnitude faster than prior methods.
why it works The winding number integral reduces algebraically to a single ray intersection and an edge sum. This exact analytical simplification achieves the best asymptotic complexity and introduces only floating-point error.
the deeper reason
The winding number—how many times a curve wraps around a point—tells you if the point is inside or outside a shape, but computing it involves an expensive surface integral. This method reduces the integral analytically to one ray-3D model intersection test plus a sum over 3D model edges, orders of magnitude faster. The construction is exact up to floating-point precision with no approximations, and it works even on broken or noisy geometry because the mathematical reduction is valid regardless, making it both faster and more robust than approximation methods.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Winding numbers — a measure of whether a point is inside a 3D shape — are used constantly in graphics and simulation. Computing them naively on large meshes is slow, but existing fast methods use approximations. The goal is to compute exact winding numbers quickly on realistic, messy meshes with gaps and overlaps.

why it's hard

The classical formula for winding numbers is a surface integral that's expensive to evaluate. Fast approximations lose accuracy on imperfect meshes. There's a tension between accuracy and speed that existing methods don't fully resolve.

what they do

They analytically simplify the surface integral to just a single ray-mesh intersection test plus an elementary sum over boundary edges. This reduction is mathematically exact (no approximation), yet vastly faster than the direct integral because it replaces billions of floating-point operations with a few geometric queries.

why it works

The mathematical reduction works because the integral has special structure: most of it cancels, leaving only the contribution from ray intersections and edges. By computing analytically what would be expensive numerically, they keep the algorithm exact while making it practical on large meshes.

the payoff

Winding number queries are orders of magnitude faster than prior methods while remaining exact (only floating-point rounding error). The algorithm is simple, debuggable, and robust even on extremely noisy or corrupt meshes, making it a practical replacement for existing approximation methods.

MorphSkein: A Shape-Changing Afterimage Display Preserving Pixel Density During Surface-Area Changes Across Troposkein-Based Shapes
uses Spinning cables form natural axisymmetric shapes governed by physics. The display maps pixels to those mathematical shapes, preserving density by adjusting how many pixels render per revolution as the shape expands.
why it works Releasing new LED rows as area grows preserves density because the rate of new rows per revolution equals the rate the troposkein expands, keeping pixels-per-unit-area constant by design.
the deeper reason
A spinning rope forms a smooth, symmetric shape (troposkein) where the surface area changes as it grows or shrinks. To keep pixel density constant across this changing surface, the system must add display pixels in proportion to the area growth. The method releases new LED rows as the surface expands and adjusts rotation speed so pixel rendering keeps pace with the spinning motion. The mathematics of the rope shape guarantees this scaling works—the troposkein geometry defines exactly how area and shape relate, letting display refresh rates match the surface changes predictably.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Imagine a display made of spinning LED strips arranged like a spinning rope—the motion creates an illusion of a 3D shape, lit up with colors. As the shape morphs (the rope gets fatter or taller), you want the displayed image to adjust but stay readable: pixels should stay roughly the same size so text and details don't become invisible.

why it's hard

Shape-changing displays (made from physical materials that morph) inherently change surface area as they move. If you want to cover a growing surface with a fixed number of LEDs, they spread out—pixel density drops, details vanish. You could add more LEDs, but then when the shape shrinks, you have too many LEDs crammed together and the display wastes energy. The challenge: keep pixel density nearly constant as the shape morphs without dynamically adding/removing LEDs.

what they do

MorphSkein uses a troposkein—a shape mathematically formed by spinning cables under centrifugal force. LEDs are arranged on flexible strips wound on rewinders. As the base rotates faster, centrifugal force pushes the cables outward, making them wider and taller. As they shift shape, the system releases new rows of LEDs from the rewinders and renders extra columns per revolution on the strips. The density is preserved horizontally (more content per spin) and vertically (more rows as the troposkein expands).

why it works

Troposkein geometry is predictable mathematically; the researchers model it and derive the relationship between rotation speed, shape, and required LED count. By releasing new rows opportunistically and increasing content per frame, they compensate for area changes. The key is getting the rotation speed right: it controls the final shape and determines how much new content to render to maintain density.

the payoff

The system maintains pixel density (1.44 px/cm²) across naturally occurring axisymmetric shapes, keeping 69.44% of reachable configurations above 90% density accuracy and shape fidelity for at least one operating speed. This enables readable, morphing LED displays for dynamic, shape-changing applications.

Mechanical Cloaking of Halftoned Imagery
uses A porous material's internal structure is designed so its effective mechanical properties (how it bends and stretches as a whole) are uniform in all directions, while its visual appearance halftones a target image. Balancing visual appearance against uniform stiffness is the core constraint.
why it works A porous structure appears mechanically uniform because the material's effective stiffness is determined by the average density and arrangement of pores at a scale much larger than individual pores. At that scale, detailed variation averages out to near-uniform behavior.
the deeper reason
The goal is to make an object look like a picture when pushed or twisted, while staying mechanically uniform and predictable. The method works because porous structures with carefully designed hole patterns scatter stress and strain in ways that average out to the same response everywhere, like a sponge that bends the same way no matter where you press it. Meanwhile, the holes themselves can be arranged (their size and shape varied locally) to reflect or absorb light in patterns that paint the target image. The principle holds because bulk mechanics depend on average material properties, not microscopic details, so uniform stress response survives even if the tiny structure looks like a picture. This decoupling—hiding complexity in arrangement while keeping the material's bulk response simple—lets designers create hidden beauty.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Imagine camouflaging an object by making it *mechanically invisible*—not hiding it to the eye, but hiding how it deforms and resists pressure. If you press on it, an observer can't tell where the hard or soft parts are. This is useful for defense, architecture, and material design.

why it's hard

You want a porous structure (like a sponge or honeycombed material) whose patterns look like a picture when you view it, but whose mechanical behavior is perfectly uniform and isotropic—responding the same way to pressure from any direction. These two goals seem opposed: a picture's contrast requires regions that look different, while uniform mechanics requires everything to behave the same.

what they do

This paper defines a parametric family of porous structures—essentially patterns of holes and solid material arranged in a specific way. The holes and walls are shaped and positioned so that even though the *visual* pattern produces high contrast (dark/light like a halftone image), the overall mechanical properties remain uniform and isotropic.

why it works

Mechanics comes from the geometry of the microstructure: which direction the walls run, how thick they are, and how they're connected. By carefully designing the hole patterns, you can create regions that *look* different but have identical mechanical stiffness in all directions because the underlying network of walls distributes load uniformly.

the payoff

You can 3D-print a part that looks like a portrait when viewed but behaves like solid, uniform steel when compressed or bent. This opens doors to camouflaged structural components, artistic sculptures with hidden properties, and new metamaterial designs for aerospace and defense.

A Precision Controlled Surface-Surface Intersection Algorithm for NURBS
uses Finding where two complex surfaces intersect and controlling precision means bounding how far the true curve strays from our approximation using curvature measures and geometric hulls that must all satisfy distance limits at once.
why it works Lipschitz continuity bounds how much a smooth curve can wiggle per step, so bounding slope bounds error from any polyline. bowl-shaped hulls contain the curve, making hull-to-polyline distance an upper bound on true error.
the deeper reason
The core task is tracing where two smooth curved surfaces intersect, then representing the intersection curve with controlled error. The principle is that a smooth curve's distance from any nearby polyline approximation is bounded by the flatness of the curve itself, which follows from smoothness guarantees like Lipschitz continuity. By choosing polyline segments short enough relative to how 'wiggly' the surface is, you make error predictable and controllable. This lets the algorithm give manufacturers watertight promises about precision—critical when parts must fit exactly.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When two 3D surfaces pass through each other (common in CAD design, 3D printing, mesh generation, and engineering simulations), you need to find the curve where they intersect precisely. If the intersection curve is wrong, manufactured parts will not fit together, simulations will diverge, or 3D prints will have gaps or overlaps.

why it's hard

The most widely used surface description in CAD is NURBS (a mathematical formula for smooth surfaces). Computing where two NURBS surfaces intersect is not straightforward—you must solve equations numerically, and the answer depends on how much precision you accept. Too coarse and parts fail; too fine and the algorithm becomes impractical.

what they do

The method leverages Lipschitz bounds and convex hulls to guarantee a maximum error in the intersection curve (Hausdorff distance), then uses a novel curve-fitting approach to measure and control the error of the final fitted curve. A fast bounding-box computation and normal-range analysis sort intersection types upfront.

why it works

The paper uses two geometric properties: Lipschitz continuity (roughly, a bound on how fast the surface changes) and convex hulls (the smallest box containing a surface patch). These bounds let you control the maximum distance between the computed polyline (piecewise-linear approximation) and the true intersection curve. By analyzing the surface normals, you can determine whether intersections exist and what type they are, which lets you handle different cases efficiently.

the payoff

The result is a certified precision control on surface-surface intersections for NURBS—the algorithm delivers an explicit guarantee on accuracy before you run it, and outperforms commercial and open-source kernels in precision, speed, and robustness.

Points as Tori: Fast Pointwise Signed Distance for Point Clouds
uses Signed distance to point clouds is computed by fitting each point with a torus (which has a closed-form distance formula), using a a system that learns from examples to predict local curvature, enabling fast queries without global optimization.
why it works Tori have closed-form signed distance formulas. Fitting them locally to point clouds gives instant analytical queries at any resolution—no grid or global optimization. Winding numbers and Poisson reconstruction both measure enclosed volume; this unifies them under tori.
the deeper reason
The core task is estimating signed distance to a point cloud—how far any query point sits from the surface and which side. The principle unifying this work is that locally fitting shapes with known analytical distance functions (like tori with simple formulas) builds a smooth approximation covering the whole point set. Because curvature at each point predicts local shape, a network can predict fitting parameters feed-forward without global optimization. This avoids expensive global solving, staying smoothly adjustable and parallelizable while giving access to applications like Boolean operations and offsets previously needing explicit surface reconstruction.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When you have a cloud of 3D points (from photos, 3D scanning, or synthetic renderers), you often want to query the signed distance at any location: how far is a point from the surface, and is it inside or outside? Signed distance fields power morphological operations (offsets, shrinking), Boolean operations (union, intersection), and shape visualization. Computing this efficiently without storing a dense grid is valuable.

why it's hard

Point clouds do not explicitly define a surface—you must infer it from the points and normals. Standard approaches either require a costly global optimization step or discretize space into a dense grid (memory-intensive). You need a method that is fast, requires no optimization, and works at arbitrary spatial resolution.

what they do

The method fits tori locally to point clouds using a neural network that predicts curvature and shift parameters feed-forward. Each point's torus contributes an analytical signed distance function. The method works directly without explicit surface reconstruction.

why it works

A torus (donut) has a closed-form, analytical formula for signed distance at any point—you just plug in coordinates and get the answer. By fitting a torus locally to each point in the cloud (using a learned neural network to predict per-point curvature and offset), you inherit the torus's analytical distance function. This is parallelizable, requires no global solve, and connects to classical reconstruction theory (winding numbers and Poisson reconstruction) which validates the mathematical foundation.

the payoff

Signed distance to arbitrary point clouds (from photos, 3D Gaussians, neural fields) can be queried at any spatial resolution instantly, enabling direct applications like surface offsetting, Boolean operations, and visualization without computing an explicit mesh.

Constant Mean Curvature Surfaces from Discrete Harmonic Maps
uses Constant mean curvature surfaces (elegant shapes with balanced bending) are built by discretizing the classical fact that their normal maps are harmonic—i.e., as smooth as possible—solved via energy minimization and Poisson equations.
why it works Smooth constant-mean-curvature surfaces have harmonic Gauss maps. Discretizing this property directly via Dirichlet energy minimization makes discrete surfaces exactly mirror the smooth theory, preserving the mean-curvature property at the grid level.
the deeper reason
The core objects are surfaces with constant mean curvature—perfectly balanced curvedness everywhere. The mathematical principle is that the curved normal directions on such surfaces satisfy a flatness equation (harmonicity) identical to how heat or steady-state charge distributes. By solving this distribution equation on a discrete 3D model, you automatically capture the structure of these rare, beautiful surfaces. Because the connection is exact—not approximate—geometric properties like conformal parameterization and closed forms flow through to the discrete version. This gives a direct, simple recipe for creating architecturally appealing shapes with guaranteed correctness.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Minimal surfaces—shapes that have the smallest surface area for their boundary—appear in physics (soap films), architecture (pavilions, domes), and art. A special case is constant mean curvature (CMC) surfaces, where the curvature is uniform everywhere; they have aesthetic appeal and appear in nature. Computing them from discrete data (points, boundary curves) is a classical challenge in discrete differential geometry.

why it's hard

CMC surfaces satisfy a differential equation based on curvature, and computing them discretely requires careful numerical approximation. Standard discretizations can be fragile, especially for boundary-value problems. There is a classical continuous theory using Gauss maps (a way to encode surface orientation) that is elegant, but translating it to discrete meshes is not straightforward.

what they do

The team discretizes CMC surfaces by requiring their Gauss maps to be harmonic, then solves a discrete Dirichlet energy minimization problem. They add a discrete Möbius-invariant energy formulation for robustness and show how to construct discrete CMC surfaces with control over boundary tangent planes.

why it works

The key insight is that the Gauss map of a CMC surface is harmonic (solves Laplace's equation, the fundamental smoothness equation). Discretizing this relationship directly via harmonic energy minimization mirrors the continuous theory exactly. By using a novel Möbius-invariant discretization of energy for sphere-valued maps, the method achieves robustness beyond standard inverse-cotangent formulations.

the payoff

CMC surfaces can be computed by solving simple, direct discretizations that remain faithful to the continuous theory, with control over boundary conditions and support for closed, periodic, and free-boundary examples.

16 papers

Adding up an infinity of tiny contributions

In graphics, nearly every quantity that matters — a pixel's color, a surface's charge, a fluid's flow — comes from an infinity of tiny contributions arriving from all directions and times at once. Light is the prime example: a single point on your screen receives photons from countless paths through the scene, scattered by materials, bouncing off surfaces, arriving over a continuous spectrum of directions. The pixel's final color is literally the sum of all those contributions. But you cannot enumerate an infinite sum. The core insight is that you never need to: most of the contribution is concentrated in a few large peaks. By breaking the infinite total into finite chunks — sampling along rays, discretizing time, chopping space into voxels — you can add up the chunks and trust that as you make the chunks smaller, your finite sum closes in on the true infinite answer. This is integration: turning an uncountable infinity into a countable approximation.

Why it works — the principle

Integration works because continuous quantities are not random and scattered; they have structure. Most of the contribution comes from regions of high density — where light is bright, where mass clusters, where forces concentrate — and these regions are finite in number and size. The guarantees come from the mathematics: any continuous total can be trapped between an over-estimate and an under-estimate (by summing tall and short rectangles under a curve), and as you chop your intervals thinner, both traps squeeze tighter and tighter toward the true answer. This squeezing never stops — you can always get closer — but you reach the 'close enough' point long before infinity. The method fails only when the quantity has wild, unbounded spikes (infinite variance), but in graphics, real physics forces smoothness: light refracts gradually, materials blend, heat diffuses. The structure of physics itself guarantees that your approximation converges.

Connecting the dots across these 16 papers

Across these 16 papers, integration appears in disguises that all share one move: formulate the answer as a continuous sum, then approximate it with samples or pieces. In volumetric rendering (GenPIE, Sample Matching, Efficient Fur), light traveling through smoke or fog is a ray integral — light lost to scattering plus light arriving from all directions — broken into small segments along the ray, evaluated stochastically. In holography (HoloPathTracer), the wavefront is an integral over all light paths; Monte Carlo path tracing randomly samples paths and weights them to approximate this path integral. In inverse problems (Thermal Non-Line-of-Sight, Gradient Domain Reconstruction), the forward model is an integral (convolution of light transport with a kernel, or a Poisson integral); the solver inverts this by treating it as an optimization over gradients or learned features, then reconstructs. In numerical PDEs (Schrödinger Bridges, Biharmonic Diffusion), the solution satisfies a differential equation (which locally sums up small changes); the solver samples random walks or boundary conditions to estimate the integral form. In imaging (Hi-SPAD, Hyperspectral), photon arrivals are random samples; their histogram is the integral of the spectrum. In geometry (Antipodal Winding Numbers), point-in-shape tests use boundary integrals expressed as ray-surface intersections; the method transforms the volume integral into a surface integral, which is far cheaper to compute. In differentiable rendering (Closed-Form Convolution, Differentiable Neutron Transport), you need the derivative of an integral; by sampling the integrand or using convolution, you can backpropagation through the sum. The single thread: pick the right decomposition of your integral, evaluate it piecewise, and sum.

picture it Imagine sunlight hitting a table. The table's color is not determined by just the one ray of sunlight coming straight down from the sun — it is the sum of light arriving from every angle: bounced off clouds, reflected from nearby walls, scattered by the air itself, each contributing a tiny bit to the final brightness you see. You cannot measure each of those infinite directions separately, so instead you point a light meter in a few key directions, measure in each, and add them up. The more directions you measure, the closer your sum gets to the true total. By noon tomorrow, when you have sampled more directions and time has smoothed out the variations, your sum will be almost exact. This is exactly what a rendering algorithm does: sample the light from many directions and times along a ray, add them up, and trust that enough samples will converge to the true color.
GenPIE: A Time-Resolved Plenoptic Imager
uses Light bounces off surfaces and through the scene in complex paths; the method uses a generative model to predict missing light paths when sensors cannot directly observe them, then refines these guesses by tracking how light actually flows.
why it works It works because decoupling light sources from detectors lets you actively probe indirect paths that self-occlusion hides, and a foundation model's geometric prior fills in blind spots — solving through generative inversion leverages strong semantic constraints to make the ill-posed sparse-sampling problem solvable.
the deeper reason
Capturing how light bounces through a scene and arrives at a camera from many angles and times is fundamentally an underdetermined problem: more information exists than can be physically observed. The approach uses a foundation model to provide a strong informed guess about the scene's 3D shape and content, which dramatically shrinks the space of plausible answers. A generative framework then refines this guess by working backward from the sparse measurements: it asks whether candidate light paths would produce the observed signals if traced through the candidate scene. A decoupled illumination and detection system allows testing many indirect light paths that standard setups cannot probe. The combination of prior knowledge and measured consistency ensures that the inferred scene, while not unique, is both plausible and physically grounded to the actual observations.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

The plenoptic function is the map of all light rays bouncing around a scene—for each direction looking from each spot, how bright is it? Capturing it fully reveals all the visual information about a scene. Time-resolved imaging (recording when light arrives) adds a temporal dimension, useful for seeing through fog or reconstructing 3D from light travel time. But sampling this huge space is practically impossible.

why it's hard

The plenoptic function has five dimensions: two for camera position, two for viewing angle, and one for time. Sampling densely requires millions of measurements. Current systems couple illumination and viewing—you turn on a light and a camera sees it, but you can't independently control where light comes from and where you observe reflections. Many indirect light paths are blocked by occlusion (a nearby object hiding a far one).

what they do

This paper decouples the laser (that sends light) from the detector (that measures it) so they can probe from different angles independently. They use a generative model—specifically, a 3D foundation model for initialization—to fill in the massive gaps from sparse samples. A differentiable transient path tracer (a physics simulator you can backpropagate through) refines the solution to match the few measurements you actually took.

why it works

Foundation models come with strong 3D geometric priors (they've seen thousands of shapes and scenes), so they make reasonable guesses for unseen regions. The differentiable path tracer then tunes those guesses to be physically consistent with what you measured. Decoupling illumination and detection means you can shine light where a wall would block direct viewing, recovering indirect paths. The combination of learned priors and physics is more powerful than either alone.

the payoff

The system captures high-dimensional light transport from sparse, practical measurements, recovering both visible and hidden regions. This enables new computational imaging: seeing through fog, capturing indirect reflections, reconstructing 3D transient phenomena. The generative framework elegantly bridges the gap between the measurements you can realistically take and the full information you want.

Gradient Domain Reconstruction for Monte Carlo PDE Solvers
uses Solving Poisson equations with random rays is slow because the answer wiggles wildly. Instead of estimating the solution itself, estimate how it changes locally (its slope), then reconstruct the answer more stably from those differences.
why it works The differences between nearby solution values have lower noise than the raw values themselves—like taking a derivative smooths out scatter. Estimating these clean differences and then reconstructing the original signal via integration recovers the solution faster because you're sampling where the noise is smallest.
the deeper reason
Solving Poisson equations (pressure fields, steady heat image-generating) using random sampling is noisy because each sample contributes spread of error independently. The key insight is to measure the answer indirectly: instead of solving for values at query points, solve for differences between nearby points. This shift works because the Poisson equation itself (the local rule) is all about how things change across space, so it already encodes difference information. By targeting differences, the method exploits a deeper correlation: errors at adjacent points are related by the equation, so they co-vary predictably and can reinforce each other when reconstructed. The mathematical guarantee comes from techniques borrowed from rendering: once the difference field is built, efficient local reconstruction recovers smooth full values without bias. This strategy cuts spread of error because you trade one high-noise direct measurement for a bundle of cheaper differences that naturally agree via the equation's structure.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Physicists and engineers often need to solve differential equations on complicated shapes—things like steady-state heat flow or electrostatics. Traditional grid-based methods struggle with complex geometries, but Monte Carlo sampling (throwing random rays) can handle any shape. The question is: can we make Monte Carlo *faster* by learning tricks from rendering?

why it's hard

Naive Monte Carlo on Poisson equations (which describe how quantities like temperature change across a domain) converges slowly because the variance is huge. Rendering has a technique called gradient-domain rendering—instead of estimating the final image directly, you estimate differences between nearby pixels and reconstruct the image. This is much faster, but it was never adapted to solve differential equations.

what they do

The authors adapt the gradient-domain idea: instead of estimating the solution at each point separately, they estimate the *difference* in the solution between nearby query locations. They also borrow reconstruction techniques from gradient-domain rendering to recover the full solution from these differences without adding extra error. The Monte Carlo sampling itself targets these local differences, which have much lower variance than the absolute values.

why it works

Differences between nearby points are intrinsically smaller and noisier than absolute values, so they converge faster with random sampling. By reconstructing the whole solution field afterward (a technique rendering developed decades ago for image denoising), you get an accurate answer from fewer samples. This is the same principle that makes gradient-domain rendering work: collaborate with math that's already efficient rather than fighting variance head-on.

the payoff

Physics simulations on complex meshes and grids converge faster with lower error. This matters for applications like acoustic simulations, electromagnetic field modeling, or fluid dynamics on irregular domains where traditional grid methods were cumbersome or inaccurate.

Schrödinger Bridges on Discretized Geometric Domains
uses Interpolate between two probability distributions by finding the smoothest path between them via entropy-regularized transport. The heat equation reveals this path; apply Sinkhorn iterations (alternating local averaging and scaling) to enforce boundary constraints.
why it works Entropy and optimal transport are secretly dual: minimizing fuzzy disorder (entropy) while moving stuff around efficiently (transport) produces the same solution either way. The heat equation couples these automatically, so alternating heat spreading with constraint corrections reaches the perfect interpolation.
the deeper reason
Interpolating between two probability distributions (like transitioning one shape into another) faces a trade-off: fast, rough interpolation or slow, smooth interpolation. Schrödinger bridges minimize entropy—a measure of how much randomness creeps into the interpolation—subject to matching start and end distributions, giving the sharpest middle ground. The mathematics connects entropy-regularized optimal transport to the heat equation, which describes how heat (or probability) spreads over time. On a discrete surface (3D model), this duality still holds: applying the discrete heat operator (which spreads probability locally) and enforcing distribution constraints iteratively converges to the minimum-entropy path. This is proven to work because the heat operator and the constraint enforcement are duals of each other mathematically—they balance like opposite forces. The practical result is sharper interpolants than competing methods: you get a smoothly varying middle state that stays close to actual data, not a blurred average, because entropy regularization penalizes unnecessary spreading.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Imagine you want to smoothly morph between two shapes, or smoothly interpolate between two distributions of color—say, black on the left fading to white on the right. You want the intermediate steps to look natural, not just fade everything to gray. Optimal transport is the right math framework, but traditional methods are slow and work poorly on the complex surfaces we actually care about.

why it's hard

Standard optimal transport methods (like Wasserstein barycenters) are expensive: they require discretizing the path between the two distributions and solving large systems. On mesh-based domains (surfaces and grids), naive discretization blows up the problem size. Even with clever tricks, the interpolations tend to blur details and don't preserve the exact boundary distributions you started with.

what they do

The authors use a classical connection between optimal transport and heat flow (Schrödinger bridges), developing a discrete version that works directly on meshes and grids. The core algorithm alternates between applying the heat kernel (standard numerical technique for diffusion) and enforcing boundary constraints via multiplicative updates. This is a direct, efficient algorithm (Sinkhorn iteration) that respects the mesh structure and avoids expensive transport computations.

why it works

The heat kernel is already well-understood for meshes (finite element methods have been using it for decades) and is cheap to compute. By grounding the math in diffusion rather than explicit transport, the method naturally handles irregular geometries. The iterative enforcement of boundary conditions means you get interpolants that exactly match the start and end distributions, not blurred approximations.

the payoff

Smooth shape morphing, color transfers, and other interpolations on complex meshes and grids now converge faster while preserving geometric detail. This is useful for animation (smooth transitions between poses), image editing (natural color transfers), and physics simulation (smooth initial conditions).

Sample Matching for Joint Extinction Gradient Estimation in Differentiable Volume Rendering
uses Rendering smoke requires summing light arriving along rays, but computing how that sum changes with smoke density is noisy. Evaluate both scattering and transmission effects at matching sample points along the same ray segment to cancel out their wild swings.
why it works Light extinction slope have two parts—scattering at one point and dimming along the ray—that have opposite signs and therefore cancel noise. Evaluating both parts at the same sample locations lets their opposite swings cancel, shrinking the spread of error without biasing the answer.
the deeper reason
Computing the downhill direction for volume rendering optimization is noisy because the renderer follows light rays through fog, and the downhill direction must account for how scattering and opacity both change when a voxel is adjusted. The key insight is that the two the downhill direction components come from different integration domains: scattering at a single bounce point, and opacity accumulated along a ray segment. Standard methods sample these separately, missing the fact that they're opposites—one positive, one negative—so they can cancel and reduce noise. Sample matching evaluates both at the same locations, which works because it couples them mathematically within a single integral formula. This is proven unbiased because the reformulation derives from the path integral calculus—the math guarantees the coupling preserves the true the downhill direction value. The efficiency gain comes from reusing light paths for multiple probe the downhill direction. This strategy reduces spread of error sharply because exploiting the negative correlation between components is free once you share sample locations—the improvement is guaranteed by statistics, not tuning.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When you render a volumetric scene (smoke, clouds, fog, the inside of a translucent object) and want to optimize it to match a target appearance, you need gradient information—how much does the final image change if you tweak the volume slightly? But computing these gradients is extremely noisy, making optimization slow. Can we reduce that noise?

why it's hard

The gradient has two conceptually different pieces: (1) how much light scatters at a particular point (a point-wise calculation), and (2) how much light gets transmitted through a segment of the volume (an integral along the ray). Existing methods sample these two pieces at different locations because they're mathematically separate. But because they have opposite signs and cancel each other, sampling them independently throws away huge negative correlations that could reduce variance.

what they do

The authors reformulate the math so both gradient components are evaluated at the *same* sample locations. Instead of treating them as separate integrals, they fold them into a single integrand. This "sample matching" principle makes the negative correlation between the two terms explicit and unavoidable. To be efficient, the method reuses partially sampled light paths from rendering and evaluates gradients at multiple probe points per segment, amortizing cost.

why it works

When two quantities have opposite signs and are negatively correlated, sampling them together at matched locations lets them cancel partially, reducing variance dramatically. This is a basic Monte Carlo principle—you want to capture negative correlations, not throw them away. The reformulation isn't mathematically new (it's still unbiased), but it exposes the correlation structure.

the payoff

Volume rendering optimization converges 5x faster with 80% lower gradient variance. This matters for inverse problems like reconstructing volumetric density from images, or optimizing volumetric effects to match live-action video in visual effects.

ToF ReSTIR: Time-of-Flight Rendering with Spatio-temporal Reservoir Resampling
uses Time-gated imaging traces light bounces that arrive within a narrow time window; simulating all bounces is expensive. Reuse previously computed bounces by warping them (via Newton's method) to satisfy the time constraints, then resample intelligently.
why it works Reusing light paths across time works only if they obey temporal gating (path length in air equals time); path-length-aware shifting adjusts reused rays to satisfy this constraint, so recycled paths stay valid and contribute signal instead of noise.
the deeper reason
Time-of-flight rendering simulates light bouncing on a precise time scale to capture transient phenomena like time-gated imaging, but this is computationally expensive because most reused light paths violate optical path-length constraints. The method applies the ReSTIR reuse strategy—recycling paths from nearby pixels—but adapts it for temporal constraints. The key fix is path-length-aware shift mapping: a geometric transformation based on Newton's method that adjusts reused paths to satisfy the optical path-length gating constraints. This works because Newton's method is proven to converge quickly to the correct path geometry when the constraint is smooth (which optical path length is). The guarantee is efficiency: paths that would otherwise be discarded now contribute signal because they're shifted into the valid temporal window. The underlying principle is that optical path length is a smooth function of position, so nearby pixels' paths can be efficiently mapped into valid ranges. This transforms an intractable reuse problem into a tractable one by respecting the physics of light timing.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Time-of-flight cameras and transient light capture measure how long light takes to travel from a camera to an object and back. This encodes incredible depth and material information, enabling new imaging modes and special effects. But simulating time-of-flight light transport is painfully slow—you need to track light that bounces at *exact* time intervals, making the rendering problem massively harder.

why it's hard

Rendering uses sophisticated path reuse techniques (ReSTIR) to speed up normal rendering by reusing light paths from one frame to the next. But time-of-flight adds an extra constraint: the total optical path length (the sum of distances light travels) must satisfy temporal gating—it must be within a specific time window. Most reused paths violate this constraint, so they become useless.

what they do

The authors adapt ReSTIR's path reuse to time-of-flight by introducing path-length-aware shift mapping. When reusing a light path, instead of using it as-is, they geometrically adjust it (using Newton's method) to satisfy the temporal gating constraint. This is inspired by techniques for rendering caustics (sparkling light patterns). The resulting framework reuses paths efficiently while ensuring they contribute valid light for the time gate.

why it works

Instead of discarding paths that violate timing constraints, the method smoothly adjusts their geometry to make them valid. Since the adjustment is geometrically principled (based on Newton's method for finding intersections), the reused paths stay physically plausible. Reusing adjusted paths means you evaluate many fewer unique light paths to get a clean result.

the payoff

Time-of-flight rendering of complex scenes with glossy and specular materials now runs at interactive rates instead of taking hours. This enables real-time simulation of advanced imaging modes, and new applications in visual effects and computational imaging.

HoloPathTracer: Fast and Accurate Wave Path Tracing for Holography
uses Holography encodes wavefronts (which waves go where) into a phase pattern—this is an integral over all light paths, analogous to rendering. Monte Carlo path tracing randomly samples paths and weights them to estimate this integral; path reuse (recycling samples) dramatically cuts the number of simulations needed.
why it works Monte Carlo solves both the rendering equation and the wave integral at once—each light ray path contributes to both radiance and phase simultaneously, converging smoothly to physical accuracy as sample count grows.
the deeper reason
Hologram phase patterns must encode the wavefront shape of a 3D scene to display it convincingly. The core problem is that traditional methods render light brightness separately from wave propagation, losing depth cues and reflection variety. By employing path tracing to collect both the brightness and the wave-optical field simultaneously, the method naturally encodes full 3D information into interference patterns. This is sound because both the rendering equation (measuring light) and the wave propagation equation (how waves combine) have the same underlying Monte Carlo solution structure—sampling paths and weighting by their contribution. A cache of ambient light further accelerates convergence, making physically accurate holograms practical.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Holographic displays promise to show 3D images with convincing depth cues and natural perspectives, without requiring glasses. But holography is fundamentally about light waves interfering, not just rays bouncing; the computer must simulate wave optics, not just standard ray-bouncing image rendering. This wave simulation is expensive and often decoupled from realistic image synthesis, limiting the visual quality of holograms.

why it's hard

Existing holographic rendering methods compute realistic radiance (light bouncing and material properties) separately from wave-optical computation (how light waves interfere and create holograms). This separation means that subtle visual effects—glossy reflections, view-dependent detail, depth continuity—get lost or approximated crudely when encoding the image as an interference pattern. The two physics simulations aren't talking to each other.

what they do

HoloPathTracer solves both the rendering equation (which rays of light bounce where and what colors they carry) and the Rayleigh-Sommerfeld integral (which governs how waves propagate and interfere) using a single Monte Carlo path-tracing algorithm. By running light paths through both computations in tandem, the method captures realistic illumination *and* faithful wave-optical encoding. A "path reuse" technique generates multiple random hologram samples with minimal extra cost.

why it works

Unified path tracing ensures that every surface reflection and material effect that matters for the radiance image also encodes into the phase hologram correctly. The wave physics and image physics stay synchronized. An ambient radiance cache (pre-computed background lighting) accelerates convergence roughly tenfold by reducing noise quickly.

the payoff

The system produces holographic phase patterns that encode 3D visual depth cues, material properties (glossy surfaces reflect coherently), and continuous view changes—all in one pass. The abstract claims "order of magnitude convergence speed improvement" via caching, meaning you can generate holograms of photorealistic scenes in practical time, not requiring trade-offs between wave fidelity and image quality.

Thermal Non-Line-of-Sight Imaging through Rough Surfaces
uses Hidden scenes emit thermal radiation that bounces through rough walls to a camera—reconstructing what's hidden requires reversing this complex light transport. The a system that learns from examples learns the convolution kernel that describes how light scatters, then inverts it to recover the hidden scene from measurements.
why it works Rough surface scattering is a convolution of the hidden scene with a spatial filter; learning that kernel explicitly from measurements narrows the space of plausible reconstructions, improving generalization over pure end-to-end learning.
the deeper reason
Seeing around walls using thermal radiation requires untangling how heat bounces off rough relay surfaces—the mixing is severe because rough surfaces scatter in many directions. The core insight models light transport through these rough surfaces as a convolution operation: the hidden scene gets blurred in a predictable way determined by surface properties. A systems that learn from examples learns this convolution local rule directly from measurements rather than solving physics equations pixel by pixel. This is mathematically sound because convolution structure is fundamental to wave and particle propagation; once the local rule is known, inverting it reconstructs the hidden scene. The learned local rule also naturally supports depth estimation, making the method robust even under severe scattering.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Non-line-of-sight (NLOS) imaging uses light bouncing off an indirect relay (a rough wall) to 'see' hidden objects around a corner—useful for rescue missions and security. Thermal NLOS imaging uses heat radiation (infrared) instead of visible light, which penetrates dust and poor visibility. The challenge is reconstructing the hidden scene from noisy thermal measurements through rough, scattering surfaces.

why it's hard

Rough surfaces (unlike shiny mirrors) scatter light and heat in complex ways. This scattering spreads signal from the hidden scene over a large area on the relay surface, and the inverse problem—recovering what the hidden scene looks like from relay measurements—becomes ill-posed and extremely sensitive to noise. Prior methods fail here because they assume relatively specular (mirror-like) reflection.

what they do

NLOSFormer is a neural network that explicitly embeds physics. It models light transport as a convolution: the hidden scene, passed through the scattering relay, produces the measured pattern. The network estimates this convolution kernel directly from the thermal measurements, using it to guide reconstruction. A companion dataset (ThermalNLOS) supports training, and a novel data augmentation strategy reduces overfitting. The network outputs both the hidden scene and relative depth cues.

why it works

By estimating the convolution kernel explicitly, the network narrows the solution space: it's not just learning "any reconstruction" but specifically one consistent with the measured light transport. The kernel acts as a learned model of how the rough surface scatters and mixes light; once estimated, it strongly constrains what reconstructions are plausible. Relative depth comes naturally from the model's understanding of how different distances map to the measurements.

the payoff

The method reconstructs hidden scenes through rough thermal relay surfaces significantly better than prior work, even in real-time for dynamic targets. This advances thermal NLOS imaging from laboratory curiosity to practical tool for seeing around corners and through difficult visibility conditions—valuable for search-and-rescue and surveillance applications.

Tensor Decomposition-Based Four-dimensional Background-Oriented Schlieren Tomography for High-Speed, High-Fidelity Flow Field Reconstruction
uses A 4D tensor (space and time) is decomposed into simpler components, then a system that learns from examples refine the solution by tracing how light rays bend through the flow field. Back-propagation updates both the tensor factors and network weights iteratively.
why it works The four-dimensional data naturally factors into three coupled pairs (XY-ZT, XZ-YT, YZ-XT), compressing it; a system that learns from examples refine this factorization efficiently, and iterative ray correction converges because each correction makes the forward model closer to the measured projections.
the deeper reason
Reconstructing fluid flow from optical images requires inverting a high-dimensional measurement problem: light bends as it passes through the fluid, creating distorted images, and you must work backward to find which fluid density explains the observed distortion. The challenge is that this inverse problem is ill-posed—many different fluids could produce similar distortions. This paper organizes the high-dimensional data (a four-dimensional space-time volume) using a decomposition principle: instead of solving for all voxels at once, it breaks the four-dimensional structure into lower-rank pieces along different splitting planes. This reduces the problem size dramatically while keeping the essential information. The underlying principle is that fluid flow evolves smoothly in space and time, so the four-dimensional representation is *low-rank*—most variation comes from a few dominant patterns repeated across space and time. By decomposing along multiple planes and refining iteratively with a systems that learn from examples, the method leverages this structure to find an accurate reconstruction efficiently.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Understanding airflow and pressure around fast-moving objects—jets, missiles, wings, wind-tunnel tests—is critical for aerospace and automotive design. Schlieren tomography is an experimental technique that uses light distortion to visualize invisible air currents. Reconstructing the full 3D flow from 2D images is a classic inverse problem, and faster, more accurate reconstruction enables real-time feedback for designers and researchers.

why it's hard

Flow fields are four-dimensional—three spatial dimensions plus time—and reconstructing them from 2D projections is mathematically underdetermined; many different 3D flows could produce the same images. The raw data from multiple camera angles is enormous and noisy, and small errors in interpreting distortions (bent light rays) accumulate into large reconstruction mistakes. Traditional methods are slow and require extensive pre-training.

what they do

The method organizes all the spatial-temporal data into a four-dimensional tensor (a multi-dimensional array) and decomposes it into components using mathematical factorization. A lightweight neural network works alongside the decomposition to correct for distortions—it learns how light rays bend in an inhomogeneous flow and iteratively adjusts the reconstruction. The computation uses mixed precision (different numerical accuracies for different steps) to speed things up without losing critical precision.

why it works

Tensor decomposition exploits the redundancy and structure in flow fields; similar flow patterns repeat spatially and over time. The neural network learns the physics of distortion directly from data rather than requiring hand-tuned models. Mixed precision keeps the computationally expensive parts fast while protecting the accuracy where it matters.

the payoff

Reconstruction runs in 12.6 seconds per frame without pre-training, a major speedup that enables real-time or near-real-time flow visualization. This makes it practical for experimental scientists to adjust their tests on the fly and for engineers to iterate on designs based on live flow data.

The Antipodal Method: Fast, Accurate, and Robust 3D Generalized Winding Numbers
uses Winding numbers (counting ray-surface intersections) are expressed as a boundary integral over the surface projected onto a sphere. This geometric insight avoids expensive surface integrals and enables fast, precise queries on both 3D models and parametric surfaces.
why it works Winding number equals ray intersections plus a boundary integral by an integral theorem; computing intersections directly (fast) and the collapsed spherical integral (small) avoids expensive surface integration while preserving the exact mathematical relationship, enabling both speed and precision.
the deeper reason
Computing whether a point is inside a closed surface is ambiguous for 3D models with holes, self-intersections, or tangled topology. The winding number is a topologically robust measure: it counts how many times the surface winds around the point. The key insight is to express winding number as two geometric pieces—ray intersections (straightforward to count) plus a boundary integral (the surface's projection onto an imaginary sphere around the point). This decomposition works because the sphere is a mathematical tool: the winding number theorem guarantees these two pieces sum to the correct answer *regardless* of how tangled the 3D model is. The efficiency gain comes from avoiding the integral: instead of integrating over the actual surface, you work with the discrete sphere projection, which has nice discretization properties. For 3D models, rays are simple geometry; for parametric surfaces, the projection is directly computable. The mathematical foundation—winding number theory—provides the guarantee that this decomposition is exact.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Determining whether a point is inside or outside a 3D shape is a fundamental operation in computer graphics, simulation, and manufacturing. You need it to check if an object has collided with something, to apply volume forces, to slice shapes in CAD software, or to prepare geometry for 3D printing. The method must work on any shape—open surfaces, self-intersecting meshes, tangled or non-manifold geometry—that real data contains.

why it's hard

Ray casting dominates the cost; the new formulation reduces it dramatically by exploiting the sphere projection structure. The boundary integral component handles tricky cases (tangent intersections, small loops) that raw ray casting misses. Precomputation and GPU implementation reach billions of queries per second.

what they do

This method expresses insidedness as the sum of two geometric quantities: the signed number of times a ray pierces the surface, and a boundary integral computed over the surface's projection onto a sphere surrounding the query point. The integral is rewritten to avoid expensive surface calculations—it becomes a 2D computation on the sphere projection instead. For meshes, this discretizes efficiently. For curved parametric surfaces, the method adapts the same principle with analytic precision.

the payoff

Speedups of 22× on CPU and 109 queries per second on GPU make generalized winding numbers practical for real-time geometry processing, large-scale simulations, and interactive design. The same algorithm works uniformly on meshes and smooth surfaces, open and closed, manifold and non-manifold—solving a long-standing robustness problem.

Hi-SPAD: Video-Rate Hyperspectral Imaging and Inference with Single-Photon Cameras
uses Single-photon detectors count random arrival times; the method reconstructs full spectra and materials by treating each detection event as a probabilistic sample. Error bounds decrease exponentially as more photon counts accumulate.
why it works SPAD counts are quantized and nonlinear, but averaging many independent photon arrivals converges to the true spectrum by the law of large numbers; error shrinks exponentially as measurement count grows.
the deeper reason
Single-photon cameras count arriving light quanta one at a time—an extremely quantized, noisy measurement—yet the goal is to recover full-spectrum images. The theoretical guarantee comes from probabilistic concentration bounds: as you accumulate more photon counts, the empirical frequency distribution of colors converges exponentially fast to the true spectral content. This works because the measurement process is unbiased (each detected photon is a fair sample of the incident spectrum) and independent; so long as you gather enough measurements, statistical law-of-large-numbers ensures the spectrum reconstructs accurately, and the error bound shrinks exponentially, making the approach sound despite the extreme quantization.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Knowing what material an object is made of—its chemical composition or reflectance properties—requires measuring not just brightness but the full spectrum of light. Today's hyperspectral cameras are bulky and slow. We want spectrum measurement fast, in video, with passive indoor lighting, using tiny sensor chips.

why it's hard

Single-photon sensors (SPAD) count individual light particles, giving extreme sensitivity and speed. But they produce quantized, nonlinear measurements with massive noise. Hyperspectral imaging normally requires many wavelength measurements per pixel. Combining both seems impossible: quantization and nonlinearity fight against spectral reconstruction.

what they do

Hi-SPAD uses a pushbroom camera design (a line of pixels that sweeps across the scene) paired with SPAD sensors arranged in a grid that captures color. The system measures how many photons arrive in each tiny time window for each color band. Despite the heavy quantization and noise, reconstruction algorithms and material classification work by leveraging the fact that errors decrease exponentially as measurements accumulate.

why it works

The key insight is that photon counting, while noisy per sample, improves steadily as you collect more measurements. With enough observations in the right setup (pushbroom plus color-sorted photon detectors), the nonlinearity becomes manageable. Theoretical analysis shows error bounds shrink exponentially, so practical reconstruction becomes feasible.

the payoff

The lab-built Hi-SPAD camera achieves hyperspectral video at 26 frames per second with 123 spectral channels under ambient lighting. The abstract validates accurate material classification and full-spectrum reconstruction through simulation and real prototypes, establishing the feasibility of photon-counting hyperspectral video in dynamic scenes.

Lifting Lines and Tone: Image-Space Stylization in Path-Space
uses Light bounces and bends as it travels through a scene; the method adds up all those paths and their effects to decide where to draw stylized lines and shading that look hand-drawn yet follow real physics.
why it works Parallel transport along path-space geometry preserves image-space structure because following the underlying surface curvature keeps lines coherent under light variation, mathematically anchored by how objects orient in 3D.
the deeper reason
Non-photorealistic styles like sketch lines live in 2D images, but light bounces in 3D. The breakthrough: geometric-driven mappings translate 2D rules into 3D language. For lines, the method uses shape curvature and ray path geometry to detect where edges should appear, preserving 2D structure as light spreads through the scene. For tones, a material-independent mapping rooted in wave physics lets artistic shading work at any point along a light path. This anchoring of image conventions to physical principles lets stylization coexist with realistic light transport.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Non-photorealistic rendering (NPR) is the art of making 3D scenes look like drawings, paintings, or stylized illustrations instead of photographs. Feature lines (the silhouettes and sharp edges you see in a sketch) and tone-based styles (hatching, stippling) are essential to the look. But modern rendering systems compute light bouncing through space (path-space rendering), and image-space stylization techniques designed for 2D don't play well with it.

why it's hard

Image-space stylization—detecting edges and hatching patterns by looking at the 2D rendered image—can produce inconsistent results under complex lighting because it treats every pixel independently. Modern physically-based rendering (the stylized rendering equation, SRE) computes light paths through space, which is powerful and accurate, but it has no native way to enforce that a feature line stays in the same place when light interacts with other objects. You need a way to lift 2D image patterns into 3D space while respecting light transport.

what they do

The researchers propose a unified framework: for any image-space stylization, establish a geometric mapping from image-space to path-space. For feature lines, they combine path-space geometry (where objects are in 3D) with a mathematical tool from differential geometry called parallel transport, which lets you slide information along a curved surface while keeping it locally consistent. This gives you a curvature-aware, stochastic line detection that lives in path-space but produces image-consistent results. For tone-based styles (hatching), they derive a canonical lifting using ideas from wave optics (stationary-phase approximations), anchored to a material-independent mapping.

why it works

Geometric mappings preserve structure: if a line is a silhouette in 3D, it should stay a silhouette when light bounces around. Parallel transport prevents distortion when the underlying geometry curves. The wave-optics motivation for tone is elegant: stationary-phase theory tells you which regions of path-space contribute most to image-space patterns, letting you do the computation where it's efficient and then project back to the image.

the payoff

The method enables image-space NPR styles to coexist with complex light transport effects—shadows, reflections, transmittance—while maintaining visual consistency. Feature lines and tone styles now interact naturally with rendering physics, enabling richer, more visually coherent non-photorealistic images.

Gaussian Point Splatting
uses Many tiny opaque points sample from each large Gaussian blur, splatting to pixels in parallel. The method solves how many points to use so their random hits blend to match the original sharp rendering.
why it works Sampling opaque points from Gaussians works because each point is independently weighted by its Gaussian's strength; averaging many random samples converges to the true blended opacity by the law of large numbers.
the deeper reason
Rendering many small blobs (Gaussians) by drawing each individually is slow when there are millions. This method samples individual points from each blob and draws them as opaque pixels in parallel using atomic hardware operations. Multiple points may land on the same pixel, but that's solved by computing how many points each blob should contribute so the final opacity matches the intended blending. Parallel threads work independently with no synchronization overhead, letting the method scale to hundreds of millions of primitives while remaining faithful to the original appearance.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Gaussian splatting is a new method for rendering 3D scenes: instead of ray-tracing (simulating light bouncing), you store the scene as a collection of blurry Gaussian shapes and paint them onto the screen. It's fast and can handle millions of small Gaussians. But the bottleneck: rendering millions of overlapping Gaussians is still slow on modern hardware, especially when you have scenes with hundreds of millions of Gaussians.
