why it works

Large language models encode world knowledge—what kitchens look like, what typically holds what—learned from text. The scene graph makes this knowledge concrete and spatial: the model can reason about "this shelf in this room" rather than abstractions. By aligning affordances with user preference (learned from context), the system balances common sense with individual choice.

the payoff

The system achieves state-of-the-art performance on detecting misplaced objects and choosing proper homes for them, in both simulated and real robotic systems. It generalizes to unseen home layouts without retraining, and improves as it observes user corrections.

LayerInbetween: Occlusion-Aware Stroke Correspondence and Inbetweening with Automatic Layering
uses Animation frames are connected by learning stroke correspondences; the system predicts hidden strokes using learned spatial transformations applied progressively across layers, smoothly interpolating missing motion.
why it works Layers explicitly represent front-to-back order, so occluded strokes can be found and placed independently. The correct layer ordering then hides them automatically in inbetweens, replacing tedious frame-by-frame work.
the deeper reason
The core challenge is drawing hidden strokes in hand-drawn animation inbetweening without explicit layering by the artist. The method predicts stroke layers across keyframes using geometric alignment cues, then generates missing strokes in the in-between frames positioned correctly behind foreground elements. This works because strokes occluded by other strokes follow predictable spatial paths—the model learns these patterns from synthetic training data where occlusion is explicit. By guessing layers first, it solves correspondence correctly even when direct matching fails. This eliminates frame-by-frame manual hiding.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

In hand-drawn 2D animation, filling in the frames between key poses (inbetweening) is tedious. When one character stands in front of another, strokes get hidden behind the foreground figure. Animators manually redraw hidden strokes, hide them, and manage layers frame by frame—laborious busywork.

why it's hard

Occlusion breaks automated inbetweening: if you don't explicitly draw a hidden stroke in both keyframes, interpolation fails. Standard approaches either ignore hidden strokes or require manual intervention for each one.

what they do

This paper introduces LayerInbetween, which predicts occlusion and handles it automatically. It learns to infer which strokes are hidden and at what layer depth, then uses a module called Global-Local Layer Transformation to guide how hidden strokes move and deform across keyframes. The system was trained on 17,000+ synthetic pairs of keyframes with known occlusions. During inbetweening, layers are resolved automatically so hidden strokes stay hidden without animator effort.

why it works

By treating the layer structure as an intermediate variable the model learns, it gains a powerful constraint on stroke motion: strokes at the same layer move coherently, and layers don't penetrate each other. This layer-aware guidance is stronger than raw pixel matching. Machine learning on synthetic data lets the system generalize to hand-drawn styles it's never seen.

the payoff

The system reliably inbetweens strokes even with occlusion, generalizing to diverse drawing styles and enabling more flexible editing of 2D animation. It removes a significant category of manual work, letting animators focus on creativity rather than frame-by-frame busywork.

EgoRelight: Egocentric Human Capture and Illumination Recovery for Relightable and Photoreal Avatar Rendering
uses The system learns to predict how light bounces off surfaces in different ways (shiny versus matte) by training on examples; specialized ray sampling identifies which nearby lit samples matter most for each viewing angle.
why it works Real materials split into fixed diffuse color and angle-dependent specular shine. Separating them means each part presents simpler patterns to the model, so each learns better than trying to learn their tangled mixture.
the deeper reason
The core task is capturing a person's full-body shape, motion, and lighting from a head-mounted camera so they appear naturally in mixed-reality spaces under new lighting. The method uses stereo depth from outward-facing cameras to reconstruct shape robustly, then learns how that person's skin scatters and reflects light separately for diffuse and glossy components. This works because separating appearance into direction-dependent and direction-independent parts exploits how real skin actually behaves—diffuse light bounces equally in all directions, while specularity depends on viewpoint. With this split, the model generalizes to new illuminations without memorizing specific lighting conditions. This lets virtual people blend seamlessly indoors or outdoors.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Imagine standing face-to-face with a holographic version of a friend, indistinguishable from real. This requires capturing their motion, inferring how light bounces off their clothes and skin, and predicting how they'd look under different lighting—all from a head-mounted camera looking outward. This is the foundation for true mixed-reality presence.

why it's hard

Existing methods solve pieces separately: capture without relighting, or require studio environments. Capturing everything simultaneously from a single viewpoint (the HMD camera) while inferring appearance under novel lighting is vastly harder than studio capture.

what they do

EgoRelight combines three components: stereo cameras on the head-mounted display capture dense depth maps, which drive an avatar mesh precisely. A neural appearance model learns to separate light-dependent shine (specularity) from permanent color (diffuse), using a specialized ray-sampling strategy that generalizes to unseen lighting without relying on rigid mathematical models of material. Finally, the system estimates the surrounding light environment as a high-dynamic-range map, so the avatar can be relit in any scene.

why it works

Depth maps give geometric ground truth, reducing ambiguity in appearance inference. Separating specular (view-dependent) from diffuse (view-independent) components makes the appearance model simpler and more transferable to new lighting. Ray-sampling strategy lets the model focus on regions that matter most perceptually, improving generalization beyond training lighting.

the payoff

The system produces photorealistic, relightable avatars from egocentric video alone—no studio needed—and renders them seamlessly in any lighting environment. This unlocks practical mixed-reality telepresence with convincing appearance.

HairGPT: Strand-as-Language Autoregressive Modeling for Realistic 3D Hairstyle Synthesis
uses Hair is modeled as a sequence of strand primitives arranged hierarchically from scalp region to fine detail; the system learns to predict each strand type autoregressively, composing complex hairstyles by chaining learned strand predictions.
why it works Hair has global organization (which strands sit where) and local detail (fine texture)—two different scales. Decoding them separately lets each part focus on its own scale without interference or confusion.
the deeper reason
The core challenge is generating realistic 3D hairstyles where global structure (how many strands, their flow direction) and local detail (thickness, waviness) must be semantically organized, not tangled into opaque texture. The method treats individual hair strands as discrete pieces and generates them autoregressively region by region from scalp to tips, building a hierarchical description from coarse layout to fine texture. This works because hairstyles, like written language, have hierarchical semantic structure—regions, strand groups, then individual strands—that humans naturally think about step-by-step. Aligning generation with that thinking makes the model interpretable and editable. This turns hair synthesis from black-box texture into authored structure.
the full first-principles story

Hair is stunning but geometrically complicated—thousands of strands, each curling and flowing. Creating realistic hairstyles by hand is expert work. You want a generative model that understands hair as a structured, semantic object (scalp regions, style layers) and can synthesize plausible variations, edit parts, and even imagine rare or fantastical styles.

why it's hard

Most generative models treat hair as texture or a continuous field, mixing global structure (which strands go where) with local texture (curl and shine). This obscures semantic organization and makes editing and control nearly impossible. You end up with pretty blur, not controllable hair.

what they do

HairGPT reformulates hair synthesis as a sequence problem: strands are generative primitives, and you build hair hierarchically, scalp region by region, from global layout to fine-grained style. It tokenizes geometry (converting 3D strand curves into discrete tokens) and uses spatial and structural decomposition—treating each scalp region separately, then building each strand from coarse (where it goes) to fine (its exact shape). This mirrors how digital artists actually groom hair.

why it works

Sequence generation naturally captures hierarchy: earlier tokens set direction, later tokens refine. Spatial and structural decomposition reduces complexity: each subproblem is simpler and more learnable. Aligning the model with artist workflow means semantics (scalp region, strand layer) emerge naturally from the learning process.

the payoff

The system generates complex, realistic hairstyles with fine detail, supports semantic editing of scalp regions and layers, and generalizes to rare styles and stylized domains. It transforms hair from opaque texture synthesis into a structured, controllable creative tool.

TwinPose: Person-Specific Subspaces for Multi-View 3D Pose Estimation
uses Multiple 2D pose detections from different camera views are reconstructed into 3D by finding poses close to all views simultaneously; the system learns person-specific subspaces so each body stays within its natural movement patterns.
why it works Grouping 3D poses by their 2D source eliminates the combinatorial explosion of which person-view pair goes with whom, leaving only the simpler geometric consistency problem within each person.
the deeper reason
The core problem is finding where multiple people are positioned in 3D when you only see 2D poses from several camera angles and must avoid forcing one giant optimization problem that gets stuck in local mistakes. The method works by inferring each person individually within their own learned pose space, where physically valid poses naturally cluster and outliers stand out. For each 2D pose seen, it finds a matching 3D twin pose in that person's private space that aligns with poses from other angles. This works because each person's body structure constrains their possible poses—their personal space is smaller and smoother than the global pose space. This makes individual fitting faster and more robust.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Capturing 3D poses of multiple people from several camera angles is useful for VFX, sports, dance. A computer has to detect where people are in each 2D image, then figure out which 2D arms and legs correspond to the same person and reconstruct their 3D skeleton.

why it's hard

Current methods treat all people's poses as a global optimization puzzle—a massive tangle of possible combinations. This makes the problem explosively complex and prone to mistakes. Additionally, they're locked to a specific 2D pose detector (OpenPose), preventing the use of newer, better detectors.

what they do

TwinPose introduces twin poses: for each person detected in 2D, create a corresponding 3D pose that inherits the 2D detector's confidence and instance information. The twins aggregate 2D joints from other camera views that are geometrically consistent with that 3D pose. All twins live in a shared 3D space, but optimization happens person-by-person in learned person-specific subspaces, not globally.

why it works

Optimizing within person-specific subspaces dramatically reduces complexity—N small problems instead of one giant tangle. Inheriting instance representation from the 2D detector lets newer detectors plug in seamlessly. Geometric consistency (rays from different cameras must intersect) is a powerful constraint that doesn't require global knowledge.

the payoff

The system works with diverse 2D pose detectors, scales to many people without explosion in complexity, and leverages richer per-instance information from modern detectors. It achieves robust, efficient multi-person 3D pose reconstruction.

Loops2Roofs: Diffusion-based 3D Roof Generation using a Loop Representation
uses Generate 3D roof geometry by iteratively denoising random vertex coordinates using a image-generating model (like reverse image-generating in image generation), guided by predicted face counts, then enforce topological consistency.
why it works Roofs are loops (closed curves) bounding polygonal faces that fit together; representing this topological skeleton lets the image-generating model learn the combinatorial rules (which loops touch which) separately from vertex coordinates, so it can reason about complex shapes across styles.
the deeper reason
Generating 3D roof geometry requires outputting valid connected shapes where edges and faces fit together without gaps or overlaps. Representing roofs as loops (cycles of edges bounding faces) is key because loops encode adjacency implicitly: each edge belongs to exactly two faces, and loops define those pairings. The image-generating model learns to gradually refine noisy random shapes into clean roofs by repeatedly predicting the direction to move corner points. This works because image-generating is proven to converge to structured outputs when trained on real examples: the model learns that certain corner point positions are more likely because they match training patterns. The neural stitching module then enforces that edge-loop closure relationships are satisfied. The guarantee comes from the loop representation itself: if faces are bounded by non-crossing loops and each edge is shared exactly twice, connectivity is automatically valid. By conditioning generation on structural priors (number of faces) and correcting topology afterward, the method ensures outputs are geometrically coherent roofs, not just point clouds.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Urban planners, game developers, and architects need to create realistic 3D building roofs—and there are infinite variations. A simple roof might have one or two slopes, but complex architecture includes dormers, valleys, ridges, and multiple levels. Generating diverse plausible roofs procedurally is hard because roofs have structure: edges must connect properly, slopes must match at corners, the whole shape has to make sense.

why it's hard

Most shape generation methods output point clouds or voxels (3D cubes), which are messy and hard to interpret as a proper roof. If you try to generate roof faces directly as polygons, you get disconnected pieces that don't form a closed surface. Existing methods either over-simplify (single sloped roof) or produce invalid topology that doesn't actually close or connect properly.

what they do

The authors represent a roof as a set of polygonal faces bounded by loops (closed curves that form edges). A neural diffusion model gradually denoises the 3D vertex coordinates while conditioning on structural priors (the number of faces and vertices per face, predicted in a separate step). After the model outputs raw coordinates, a neural stitching module fixes the topology: it enforces that edges connect properly, adjacent faces align, and the whole mesh forms a valid closed surface. A diffusion model learns what realistic roof distributions look like, and the stitching module ensures the output is geometrically valid.

why it works

Representing roofs as loops of polygonal faces is flexible enough to handle complex architecture yet constrained enough that you can enforce connectivity. Diffusion models are excellent at learning complex distributions from examples. By separating the learning (what do realistic roofs look like?) from the constraint enforcement (does this roof actually close?), each part becomes tractable.

the payoff

Game engines and urban modeling tools can now generate diverse, realistic roofs for thousands of buildings in procedural cities. The method handles everything from simple houses to complex architectural styles, and the output is always valid geometry ready to render or simulate.

Skinned Motion Retargeting with Spatially Adaptive Interaction Guidance
uses Moving one character's motion to another requires matching spatial relationships (like hand-to-face proximity). Adaptively reposition anchor points on the target body via learned prediction, then retarget motion to preserve those adaptive relationships.
why it works Static contact anchors break when limbs are short or long; dynamic anchors project onto the target body, ensuring any guidance is geometrically feasible. Structured anchors from the source skeleton ensure the spatial logic is preserved while being adapted to the new shape.
the deeper reason
Retargeting motion between characters with different body shapes requires preserving where hands touch bodies and how close limbs stay—spatial relationships that anchor movement intention. Static correspondence points fail when target bodies are exaggerated because the intended regions move. The method adapts anchor points dynamically to reachable locations on the target body using a neural predictor that learns how poses shift contact zones. The guarantee rests on two principles: first, reachability is geometric—a limb can only contact surface locations it can stretch to, and this constraint defines a valid subspace for each pose. Second, the autoencoder operating in graph space learns that target skeletons move in coordinated patterns reflecting the source's structure, so predicted motions satisfy both reachability and intent. The soft projection keeps anchors on actual surface geometry, so the learned correspondence is always real. By optimizing anchors and motion together, the framework maintains spatial semantics (the what) while accommodating shape differences (the how), respecting the principle that intent and geometry must coexist.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

When you move an animation from one character to another (like transferring a hand-gesturing animation from a tall actor to a short actor), you want to preserve the *meaning* of the motion. A hand that touches the face should still touch the face, not pass through it or gesture in empty air. This is motion retargeting, and it's hard when the target character has very different proportions.

why it's hard

Simple retargeting just rescales the skeleton to match the target character's limb lengths. But if the target is much shorter, hands reach only to the hip instead of the face—the semantic meaning (hand touching face) is lost. Geometry-aware methods fix this by defining correspondence points ("this source region maps to this target region"), but these correspondences are static. If the target has exaggerated proportions, the fixed correspondences become unreachable.

what they do

Instead of static correspondence points, this method dynamically repositions anchor points (semantic points being transferred) to lie on reachable regions of the target character. A neural network predicts how much to shift each anchor, and soft projection ensures shifted anchors stay on the target's surface. The motion retargeting then uses these dynamically adapted anchors as semantic guides. Because anchors move per-frame based on the source pose, they always point to achievable regions on the target.

why it works

By decoupling anchor placement from motion retargeting, the method can adapt anchors to reflect what's physically possible for the target character while still maintaining semantic meaning. The neural network learns to recognize which regions of the source character matter most for interaction (hand touching face) and translates that to the target's most analogous reachable location. Dynamic repositioning avoids the brittleness of static correspondences.

the payoff

Animators can retarget motion to stylized or exaggerated characters—cartoony proportions, extreme sizes—while preserving the intent of the original animation. This is valuable for game engines and film, where you want rich animations to apply across a diverse cast of characters.

Generative Modeling with Orbit-Space Particle Flow Matching
uses Generating point clouds means learning curved paths in high-dimensional space. Exploit particle permutation symmetry and embed geometric structure in the flow's final velocity so the model learns simpler, straighter paths that produce normals as a byproduct.
why it works Particles are anonymous—swapping labels changes nothing, so optimizing per-index flows wastes effort on spurious variation. Working in orbit space (treating swaps as equivalences) cuts that waste and learns simpler flows; terminal velocities in 3D space directly encode geometry like surface normals as a free byproduct.
the deeper reason
Generating particle systems (clouds, sand, deformable shapes) faces an indexing problem: assigning fixed labels to particles inflates the learning task because moving a particle from position A to B looks like many different trajectories depending on labels. The solution is orbit-space canonicalization: ignore labels and represent states up to permutation symmetry, so the same physical configuration has one canonical form. This works because the learning target itself has the same symmetry—physically identical configurations should map to identical hidden spaces. Geometric probability paths extend the flow framework by tracking not just positions but surface normals as byproducts, which constrains the flow to realistic surfaces. Arc-length-aware terminal velocities ensure the flow's endpoint respects the physical geometry. The guarantee rests on symmetry theory: if the model respects permutation symmetry and geometric structure, its learned flows automatically do too, reducing what the network must memorize. The practical result is dramatic: minimizing per-index spread of error via orbit space reduces metric error because the model operates directly on the symmetric space where particle systems truly live.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Generating 3D shapes made of particles—like point clouds, or collections of points that represent surfaces—is increasingly important for graphics and physics. But particles are fundamentally anonymous: swapping two particles' positions shouldn't change the shape, yet machine learning models often treat them as ordered lists, introducing artificial asymmetry that makes training harder.

why it's hard

Standard generative models (like diffusion models or VAEs) treat each point as a separate numbered entity. If you shuffle the point indices, the model sees a completely different training example even though it represents the same geometric shape. This inflates the variance the model has to learn, making training slower and results worse. The learning path from noise to shape is also often curved and hard to follow.

what they do

The authors build a particle-native flow-matching framework that respects permutation symmetry (the mathematical fact that swapping particle labels doesn't matter). Three key pieces: (1) canonicalize the terminal distribution so particles have canonical roles; (2) use particle embeddings so the network can specialize roles if needed; (3) design the probability path with arc-length-aware terminal velocities that respect 3D geometry. As a bonus, the terminal velocity naturally encodes surface normals as a byproduct of the flow.

why it works

By eliminating artificial permutation asymmetry from the training data, you reduce the learning problem's variance dramatically. Canonicalization means the model always sees shapes in a consistent orientation. Arc-length-aware terminal velocities ground the abstract learning process in physical geometry—instead of arbitrary high-dimensional vectors, the model is learning physically meaningful quantities.

the payoff

Generative models for point clouds now converge faster (5x fewer denoising steps), use fewer parameters (26x fewer for airplane shapes), and naturally produce surface normals without separate computation. This is useful for 3D object generation, shape synthesis, and point-based rendering.

PAColorHolo: A Perceptually-Aware Color Management Framework for Holographic Displays
uses Holographic displays distort colors due to laser light, optical imperfections, and hardware biases. The system maps input color to output color via learned neural functions; adaptive illumination adjusts light intensity, and coordinate changes (color space transformations) straighten out systematic distortions.
why it works Holographic color distortion is a composition of known optical and sensor nonlinearities; modeling each source (laser coherence, modulator limits, aberration, camera bias) and correcting it jointly recovers perceptual fidelity.
the deeper reason
Holographic displays use coherent laser light and special optical elements that distort color in complex, system-dependent ways. The core challenge is mapping user intent (desired colors) through all these transformations to achieve what the eye actually perceives. By modeling the full chain—input color space, display optics, and camera response (using learned neural models)—the system finds corrections at each stage. This is mathematically sound because the transformations are invertible or close to it: perceptual color is deterministic given the display state, so controlling inputs reliably produces outputs. Adaptive illumination then tunes the laser intensity, reducing distortion further and making color fidelity achievable despite hardware imperfections.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Holographic displays produce 3D images with excellent depth perception, but getting colors to look right is surprisingly difficult. The laser light, the liquid-crystal modulators, optical aberrations, and camera artifacts all conspire to twist colors—red comes out as orange, greens desaturate. A perceptually accurate holographic display requires careful end-to-end color management, not unlike color grading in film.

why it's hard

The challenge is that holography introduces multiple layers of color distortion: the coherent laser illumination (monochromatic light sources) mixes differently than white light; the spatial light modulator has unequal efficiency across wavelengths; chromatic aberration (different colors bend by different amounts through the optics) smears the image; and the camera used to measure the display's output introduces its own color bias. No single correction suffices—you need a system.

what they do

PAColorHolo combines four techniques. Input color space transformation maps the desired RGB to a representation suited to the coherent laser system. Adaptive illumination control adjusts laser power and timing to compensate for wavelength-dependent losses. A neural network learns the camera's color response (how it maps the actual light spectrum to RGB), inverting that mapping so the captured colors match intent. Experiments verify the approach through simulations, optical tests, and a user study where people judge color fidelity.

why it works

Neural networks excel at learning complex, non-linear mappings like "what does this light spectrum look like through a camera?" Once trained on diverse lighting and material samples, they generalize to novel scenes. Addressing color distortion at multiple stages—input, illumination, perception—means you're not fighting a single-point bottleneck but systematically trimming all sources of error. The perceptual framing (not just matching RGB values, but matching what the eye sees) ensures that the result *looks* right.

the payoff

Users report substantially improved color fidelity in both simulation and real optical experiments, confirmed by controlled user studies. This breakthrough clears a major hurdle for holographic displays as practical displays for AR/VR applications—perception of color is closely tied to perceived realism, and now that gap closes.

Fast and Accurate Gaussian Process Modelling of Real-World Materials
uses A Bayesian regression with Gaussian processes fits material reflectance properties by learning distances between observed and modeled reflectance. A custom kernel better captures reflectance features than standard kernels, enabling compact analytical representations.
why it works A Bayesian prior encodes that nearby BRDF measurements are correlated; the distance-based covariance kernel captures smoothness in material properties, so the posterior mean predicts unseen values accurately without overfitting, extracting maximum information from sparse samples.
the deeper reason
Storing material appearance (how surfaces reflect light) compactly is hard because real materials have complex, high-dimensional variation—they scatter light differently depending on viewing angle and material roughness. This paper applies Bayesian statistics: rather than learning arbitrary values, it assumes appearance follows a smooth, probabilistic pattern (a prior belief that nearby measurements should be similar). Using a Bayesian approach means the model automatically balances fitting the measured data against staying smooth, avoiding overfitting. The specific choice of local rule (a distance-based covariance function) matches the actual structure of material properties—they vary smoothly, and this local rule captures that smoothness better than generic alternatives. The mathematical guarantee comes from Bayesian theory: the posterior is provably the best balance between data fidelity and simplicity given that prior. Adapting the input structure efficiently (organizing observations hierarchically) lets the method scale: the smoothness prior compresses what would otherwise be a massive table of measurements into a compact, analytic formula.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Materials reflect light in complex, distinctive ways—brushed aluminum looks different from polished gold, and silk cloth differs from rough leather. Capturing and representing this appearance mathematically is crucial for photorealistic rendering and material design. A compact representation enables real-time rendering while a flexible one adapts to any material type.

why it's hard

Traditional methods either require many samples per material (expensive to measure and store) or use rigid mathematical models that don't capture real-world complexity. Modern measurements produce high-dimensional data (viewing angle, illumination angle, wavelength), and standard interpolation methods either bloat the representation or smooth away important fine structure like specular peaks and anisotropic patterns.

what they do

This method uses Bayesian regression with a Gaussian process—a statistical framework that treats the material's reflectivity as a smooth function and learns it from sparse samples. Instead of the usual Gaussian kernel, it uses a generalized distance covariance kernel specifically designed to preserve fine features in material appearance (like sharp specular highlights). The data is organized cleverly so that factorization dramatically reduces the dimensionality, making the computation tractable—fitting and rendering both reduce to matrix operations.

why it works

Gaussian processes provide a principled uncertainty estimate; they know which regions of the material's properties are well-constrained and which are not. The custom kernel respects the structure of material appearance better than generic smoothing. Factorization exploits the inherent separability in material data (viewing angle decouples from illumination angle in certain coordinate systems), avoiding the full curse of dimensionality.

the payoff

A complete BRDF representation fits in seconds rather than hours or days, is compact enough for real-time rendering, and adapts its complexity to match the material's actual complexity. The representation converts easily to standard rendering formats, making it plug-and-play for existing graphics pipelines. Different materials—isotropic brushed metals, anisotropic fabrics, pearls—all work uniformly.

uses Model 350k motion clips as points in a learned hidden space where similar poses cluster; at runtime, pick motion from the cluster matching current velocity and style commands, interpolating smoothly between primitives.
why it works Modular latent codes compress motion variety into a small trainable space; primitives map high-level intent cleanly to this space. This avoids the combinatorial explosion of generating every detail from scratch.
the deeper reason
Real-time game animation demands both the quality of machine learning models and the speed of hand-coded motion pieces. MotionBricks splits this problem: a single learned model handles the core motion generation task efficiently, then 'smart primitives'—plug-in modules for steering and interaction—provide the multi-faceted control that game designers need without retraining. By modularizing learned generation and leaving intuitive control to interpretable primitives, the system scales to 350,000 motion variations at interactive frame rates while staying designer-friendly.

In video games, movies, and robotics, characters need to move in real-time with precise control — walk faster, face a direction, reach for an object. Generative AI can synthesize convincing motions, but it's slow and hard to control in the fine-grained, multi-faceted way that production pipelines need.

why it's hard

Generative models trained on massive motion datasets can produce diverse and realistic motion, but they require seconds of computation. Real-time demands milliseconds. Existing models also respond poorly to multi-modal control: velocity commands, style choices, and exact keyframes simultaneously, which production rarely addresses.

MotionBricks uses a large modular neural backbone trained on 350,000 motion clips that generates motions in a learned 'latent' space (compressed, efficient representation). why it works

The system generates diverse motions in real-time (critical for interactive apps), supports multi-modal control (velocity, style, keyframes), and works on new tasks zero-shot (without fine-tuning). It bridges the gap between research generative methods and production demands for control and speed.

NeuBase: Spline Surfaces with Neural Basis Functions
uses A control 3D model defines a smooth surface; neural basis functions augment classical subdivision with learned local deformations. The design preserves four key properties—linearity, locality, smoothness, and affine invariance—ensuring direct manipulation without retraining.
why it works Neural basis functions inherit geometric properties (linearity, locality, smoothness, equivariance) from the Catmull-Clark base surface they offset, enabling direct real-time manipulation without retraining.
the deeper reason
Deforming 3D surfaces interactively requires both fitting detail and enabling direct manipulation without retraining. The approach uses a base subdivision surface (which has known, clean algebraic properties) plus an offset field learned via neural basis functions anchored to that base. This works because subdivision surfaces obey linearity, locality, and smoothness by design: any linear combination of control point displacements produces a proportional surface change, and the offset field inherits these properties through its base, so you can deform the 3D model directly via the control points without retraining while still capturing fine surface details in the learned offset.
the full first-principles story
the big picture

Designing surfaces by hand is tedious—you move control points, waiting for the surface to update each time. Real-time deformation, where dragging a handle smoothly deforms the surface instantly, is what you want. Neural networks can fit surfaces to data beautifully, but they're black boxes that resist direct manipulation.

why it's hard

Neural parametric surfaces fit detail well but break key geometric properties. Moving a control point should move only nearby surface—local control—and shouldn't break smoothness or require retraining. Neural networks globally couple all parameters to all outputs, so local intuitive edits are impossible.

what they do

NeuBase pairs a traditional subdivision surface base (which has local control and smoothness built in) with an offset field defined by neural basis functions. The neural basis functions are encoded via a learned map. This hybrid approach lets the surface fit detail via neural learning while preserving the desirable properties of classical subdivision surfaces.

why it works

By construction, the method maintains linearity (the surface depends linearly on control weights), locality (each control point influences only nearby surface), smoothness (inherited from subdivision), and affine equivariance (rotating the control mesh rotates the surface). These properties are not learned; they're architectural. So direct manipulation works without retraining.

the payoff

NeuBase supports intuitive real-time surface deformation: designers deform the control mesh and see results instantly. The surface achieves better fitting accuracy than other neural parametric representations while staying efficient even for complex shapes with dense control meshes. It balances neural expressiveness with classical geometric guarantees.
